Growth diagram proofs for the Littlewood identities Thanks: This research was funded in whole by the Austrian Science Fund (FWF) doi:10.55776/P34931 and doi:10.55776/P36863 .
Florian Schreier-Aigner

Abstract
. The (dual) Cauchy identity has an easy algebraic proof utilising a commutation relation between the up and (dual) down operators. By using Fomin’s growth diagrams, a bijective proof of the commutation relation can be “bijectivised” to obtain RSK like correspondences. In this paper we give a concise overview of this machinery and extend it to Littlewood type identities by introducing a new family of relations between these operators, called projection identities. Thereby we obtain infinite families of bijections for the Littlewood identities generalising the classical ones. We believe that this approach will be useful for finding bijective proofs for Littlewood type identities in other settings such as for Macdonald polynomials and their specialisations, alternating sign matrices or vertex models.



1.
Introduction

The Robinson–Schensted–Knuth correspondence (RSK) is a combinatorial algorithm with many applications in various areas of mathematics including combinatorics, representation theory, geometry, and probability theory.
The algorithm was first described by Robinson
[
18
]
for permutations and independently by Schensted
[
19
]
for words; the connection between these algorithms was pointed out by Schützenberger. In a seminal paper Knuth
[
11
]
extended the previous correspondence to a bijection between matrices with non-negative entries and pairs of semistandard Young tableaux of the same shape, called RSK.
In particular RSK gives a combinatorial proof of the Cauchy identity for Schur functions

(1)

\sum_{\lambda}s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x})s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{y})=\prod_{i,j}\frac{1}{1-x_{i}y_{j}}.

By restricting to the squarefree monomial
x_{1}\ldots x_{n}y_{1}\ldots y_{n}
on both sides of (
1
) we obtain the famous identity

(2)

\sum_{\lambda\vdash n}(f_{\lambda})^{2}=n!,

where
f_{\lambda}
is the number of
standard Young Tableaux
(SYTs) of shape
\lambda
.
There are two important variations of RSK: ‘‘the’’
1
1

1

Actually Burge
[
4
]
introduces four correspondences where usually the bijection introduced in
[
4
, Section 3]
is referred as the Burge correspondence.
Burge correspondence
[
4
]
which yields another bijective proof of (
1
) and dual RSK (
{\rm RSK}^{*}
), introduced by Knuth
[
11
]
, which gives a bijective proof of the dual Cauchy identity

(3)

\sum_{\lambda}s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x})s_{\lambda^{\prime}}(\mathbf{y})=\prod_{i,j}(1+x_{i}y_{j}).

The fact that RSK and its variations yield bijective proofs of these identities can be seen as one of the reasons that they have many applications. In particular all of the above identities have a representation theoretic interpretation.
For
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
and
\mathbf{y}=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{m})
the right hand side of (
1
) and (
3
) are the characters of the
{\rm GL}_{n}(\mathbb{C})\times{\rm GL}_{m}(\mathbb{C})
-module
\bigvee\left(\mathbb{C}^{n}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m}\right)
and
\bigwedge\left(\mathbb{C}^{n}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{m}\right)
respectively while the left hand sides are their decompositions into irreducible characters.
Similarly for (
2
), the right hand side is the dimension of the group algebra
\mathbb{C}[S_{n}]
while the left hand gives a formula for the dimension of its decomposition
\bigoplus_{\lambda\vdash n}(S^{\lambda})^{f_{\lambda}}
into irreducible representations of
S_{n}
.
There exist many variations of the above identities by considering representations with respect to different groups or algebras, or by regarding different families of tableaux respectively. In particular these different settings led to variations as well as generalisations of RSK; see for example
[
2
,
5
,
10
,
14
,
16
,
17
,
20
,
21
,
22
,
23
]
.

In statistical physics and probability theory the Cauchy identity has another interpretation. A partition
\lambda
is interpreted as a one dimensional particle configuration using its Maya diagram where the particle configuration corresponding to
\lambda
appears with probability proportional to
s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x})s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{y})
, see for example
[
15
]
.
As a consequence, the Cauchy identity (
1
) yields a formula for the partition function of this system. It turns out that RSK can thereby be used to study certain stochastic systems such as TASEP. In the last decades many (probabilistic) generalisations of (dual) RSK and its specialisations were introduced to study further probabilistic models, compare for example with
[
3
,
8
,
13
]
and references within. Typically these generalisations of RSK yield proofs for Cauchy identities of symmetric polynomials or symmetric functions generalising Schur polynomials including
q
-Whittaker polynomials, Hall–Littlewood polynomials or Macdonald polynomials.

A family of identities closely related to the (dual) Cauchy identity are the Littlewood identities

(4)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\\
\lambda^{\prime}\text{ even}\end{subarray}}s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\frac{1}{1-x_{i}x_{j}},

(5)

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda}s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{1-x_{i}}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\frac{1}{1-x_{i}x_{j}},

(6)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\\
\lambda\text{ even}\end{subarray}}s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{1-x_{i}^{2}}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\frac{1}{1-x_{i}x_{j}},

(7)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\\
\text{ $1$-asymmetric}\end{subarray}}s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}(1+x_{i}x_{j}),

(8)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda\\
\text{ $-1$-asymmetric}\end{subarray}}s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{n}(1+x_{i}^{2})\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}(1+x_{i}x_{j}).

Note that (
5
) is often called “the” Littlewood identity. These identities have bijective proofs which are based on RSK, the Burge correspondence and dual RSK. The identity (
5
) follows immediately by a symmetry property of RSK
[
11
, Theorem 3]
or the Burge correspondence respectively, the identities (
4
) and (
6
) need a closer analysis of RSK
[
11
, Theorem 4]
. The first bijective proofs for (
7
) and (
8
) were presented by Burge
[
4
]
.
Again there is a connection to representation theory, in the case of the Littlewood identities this is by the Weyl denominator formula.

While RSK is usually described as an insertion algorithm, there are various different descriptions such as Fulton’s
matrix-ball construction

[
9
]
building on Viennot’s shadowing
[
24
]
, or most prominently Fomin’s description by using
growth diagrams

[
6
,
7
]
. Fomin’s construction builds on two key observations. Firstly, we can interpret both sides of the Cauchy identity as certain pairs of chains in Young’s lattice. The left hand side of (
1
) is a weighted sum over pairs of chains
((P^{(i)})_{i},(Q^{(j)})_{j})
of the form
2
2

2

For simplicity we explain the case of symmetric polynomials, i.e., the restriction to
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
and
\mathbf{y}=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{m})
.

(9)

\emptyset=P^{(0)}\prec\cdots\prec P^{(n)}=\lambda=Q^{(m)}\succ\cdots\succ Q^{(0)}=\emptyset

and the right hand side of (
1
) is the shown product times the weight of the trivial pair
((P^{(i)})_{i},(Q^{(j)})_{j})=((\emptyset)_{i},(\emptyset)_{j})
which satisfies

(10)

\emptyset=Q^{(m)}\succ\cdots\succ Q^{(0)}=P^{(0)}\prec\cdots\prec P^{(n)}=\emptyset.

For precise definitions see Section
2
.
The second observation is that we can inductively transform each pair of chains of the first form into the trivial pair of chains by using
local growth rules
and thereby gain the wanted product of the right hand side of (
1
).

Both observations can be reformulated algebraically by using up and down operators. Firstly we can express the Schur polynomial
s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x})
(resp.,
s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{y})
) by using up operators
U_{x_{i}}
(resp., down operators
D_{y_{j}}
).
This implies that the weighted sum over all pairs of chains of the form as in (
9
) is equal to

(11)

\left\langle D_{y_{1}}\cdots D_{y_{m}}U_{x_{n}}\cdots U_{x_{1}}\emptyset,\emptyset\right\rangle,

while the right hand side of (
1
) times the weight of the trivial chain as in (
10
) is equal to

(12)

\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq n\\
1\leq j\leq m\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{1-x_{i}y_{j}}\left\langle U_{x_{n}}\cdots U_{x_{1}}D_{y_{1}}\cdots D_{y_{m}}\emptyset,\emptyset\right\rangle.

The second observation can be reformulated as a commutation relation of the operators
U_{x_{i}}
and
D_{y_{j}}
stating that we obtain a factor
(1-x_{i}y_{j})^{-1}
when commuting these operators.
It is not difficult to see that (
12
) can be obtained from (
11
) by iteratively commuting all up with all down operators. In order to obtain a bijective correspondence for the Cauchy identity, it therefore suffices to find a bijective proof of the commutation relation which is typically called local growth rules.
In the case of the (dual) Cauchy identity for Schur polynomials, this approach leads to an infinite family of bijective proofs.
More generally, Fomin’s approach yields a framework for (bijective) proofs of Cauchy type identities with many possibilities for generalisations by choosing different posets or weights, i.e., define different up and down operators. This approach was used for example by Bufetov and Matveev
[
3
]
to obtain a combinatorial proof of the Cauchy identity for Hall–Littlewood polynomials or in
[
8
]
by Frieden and the author to obtain a generalisation of dual RSK for Macdonald polynomials.

The motivation for this article is twofold. Firstly we present a concise summary of Fomin’s framework for both the Cauchy and dual Cauchy identity for Schur polynomials and how it relates to the previous work by Knuth and Burge. For the case of the Cauchy identity this was already done by van Leeuwen
[
23
]
, however the approach there is to define the local growth rules for RSK recursively by using the local growth rules of the Robinson correspondence, i.e., the restriction of RSK to permutations, while we give a direct description.

The second goal of this paper is to extend the framework of growth diagrams such that it is applicable to Littlewood identities. As in the Cauchy case, the approach is a two step process. In the first step we obtain an algebraic proof by introducing new relations between the up and down operators which we call
projection identities
. In a second step we provide bijective proofs for these identities which become the building blocks for the bijective proofs of the Littlewood identities.

The paper is structured in the following way. In Section
2
we present Fomin’s framework for the (dual) Cauchy identity. In particular in Section
2.2
we introduce the up and down operators, their commutation relations and present bijective proofs thereof, i.e., the (dual) row and column insertions. In Section
2.3
(dual) growth diagrams are introduced. We show how they yield a bijective proof of the (dual) Cauchy identity and how the typical insertion descriptions of RSK can be obtained from local growth rules. In Section
2.4
we show how this framework can be extended easily for the skew (dual) Cauchy identity and how one can obtain the (dual) Pieri rule as an immediate consequence.
In Section
3
we extend the framework to Littlewood identities by presenting the projection identities and their combinatorial proofs in Section
3.1
. In Section
3.2
we define triangular (dual) growth diagrams and show how they yield bijective proofs for the Littlewood identities.
In Section
4
we present the general framework for up and down operators and how Cauchy and Littlewood type identities follow from commutation relations and projection identities between these operators.

2.
The Cauchy identity and its dual

2.1.
Preliminaries

A
partition

\lambda
is a weakly decreasing sequence of positive integers
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{k})
. We call
k
the
length
of
\lambda
and the
\lambda_{i}
its
parts
. We say that
\lambda
is a partition of
n=|\lambda|=\lambda_{1}+\cdots+\lambda_{k}
which is denoted by
\lambda\vdash n
. The
Young diagram
of
\lambda
is a collection of left-justified boxes, also called
cells
, where the
i
-th row from bottom
3
3

3

Note that we are using French convention; for English convention one replaces “bottom” by “top”.
has
\lambda_{i}
boxes. As usual we identity a partition with its Young diagram. The
conjugate

\lambda^{\prime}=(\lambda_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,\lambda_{m}^{\prime})
of a partition
\lambda
is obtained by reflecting the Young diagram of
\lambda
along the diagonal
x=y
. For a partition
\lambda
let
l=l(\lambda)
be the length of the
Durfee square
which is given by
l(\lambda)=\max_{i}(\lambda_{i}\geq i)
. The
Frobenius notation
of a partition
\lambda
is defined as the pair
(\lambda_{1}-1,\ldots,\lambda_{l}-l|\lambda_{1}^{\prime}-1,\ldots,\lambda_{l}^{\prime}-l)
.

Example 2.1
.

The partition
\lambda=(6,5,3,3,1)
with Frobenius notation
(5,3,0|4,2,1)
and its conjugate
(5,4,4,2,2,1)
with Frobenius notation
(4,2,1|5,3,0)
are shown on the left and right respectively. In both cases the cell
(4,2)
is marked in blue; note that we use Cartesian coordinates for cells.

A filling of the cells of
\lambda
with positive integers is called

•

a
semistandard Young tableau (SSYT)
of shape
\lambda
if the entries are weakly increasing along rows and strictly increasing along columns,

•

a
dual semistandard Young tableau
of shape
\lambda
if the entries are strictly increasing along rows and weakly increasing along columns.

We denote by
{\rm SSYT}_{\lambda}(n)
the set of semistandard Young tableaux of shape
\lambda
with entries at most
n
and by
{\rm SSYT}_{\lambda}^{*}(n)
the set of dual SSYTs of shape
\lambda
and entries at most
n
. It is immediate that SSYTs of shape
\lambda
and dual SSYTs of shape
\lambda^{\prime}
are in bijection by conjugating the Young diagram together with its entries. For a (dual) SSYT
T
we denote by
\mathbf{x}^{T}
the
weight
of
T
which is defined as

\mathbf{x}^{T}=\prod_{i\geq 1}x_{i}^{\#(i\text{ entries in }T)}.

Example 2.2
.

The following are a semistandard Young tableau (left) and a dual semistandard Young tableau (right), both of shape
(5,4,2,1)
and with weight
x_{1}x_{2}^{3}x_{3}^{2}x_{4}^{2}x_{5}^{4}
.
\vphantom{1}\smash{1}
\vphantom{1}\smash{2}
\vphantom{1}\smash{2}
\vphantom{1}\smash{4}
\vphantom{1}\smash{5}
\vphantom{1}\smash{2}
\vphantom{1}\smash{3}
\vphantom{1}\smash{3}
\vphantom{1}\smash{5}
\vphantom{1}\smash{4}
\vphantom{1}\smash{5}
\vphantom{1}\smash{5}
\vphantom{1}\smash{1}
\vphantom{1}\smash{2}
\vphantom{1}\smash{3}
\vphantom{1}\smash{4}
\vphantom{1}\smash{5}
\vphantom{1}\smash{2}
\vphantom{1}\smash{3}
\vphantom{1}\smash{4}
\vphantom{1}\smash{5}
\vphantom{1}\smash{2}
\vphantom{1}\smash{5}
\vphantom{1}\smash{5}

For a sequence
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
of variables, we define the
Schur polynomial

s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x})
as

s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{T\in{\rm SSYT}_{\lambda}(n)}\mathbf{x}^{T}.

The following is a fundamental theorem in the theory of symmetric polynomials.

Theorem 2.3
.

Let
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
and
\mathbf{y}=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{m})
be two sequences of variables. Then

(13)

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda}s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x})s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq n\\
1\leq j\leq m\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{1-x_{i}y_{j}},

\displaystyle(\text{Cauchy identity})

(14)

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda}s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x})s_{\lambda^{\prime}}(\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq n\\
1\leq j\leq m\end{subarray}}(1+x_{i}y_{j}),

\displaystyle(\text{dual Cauchy identity})

where both sums are over all partitions
\lambda
.

Both identities can be interpreted as identities between two generating functions as follows. The Cauchy identity is equal to

\sum_{\lambda}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P\in{\rm SSYT}_{\lambda}(n)\\
Q\in{\rm SSYT}_{\lambda}(m)\end{subarray}}\mathbf{x}^{P}\mathbf{y}^{Q}=\sum_{A=(a_{i,j})}\prod_{i,j}(x_{i}y_{j})^{a_{i,j}},

where the left hand side is over all partitions
\lambda
and the right hand side is over all
n\times m
matrices
A
of non-negative integers.
The dual Cauchy identity on the other hand is equal to the identity

\sum_{\lambda}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P\in{\rm SSYT}_{\lambda}(n)\\
Q^{*}\in{\rm SSYT}^{*}_{\lambda}(m)\end{subarray}}=\sum_{B=(b_{i,j})}\prod_{i,j}(x_{i}y_{j})^{b_{i,j}},

where the left hand side is over all partitions
\lambda
and the right hand side is over all
n\times m

\{0,1\}
-matrices. We use this interpretation in Section
2.3
to provide a combinatorial proof.

2.2.
Up and (dual) down operators

In this section we present an elementary algebraic framework which allows us to prove Theorem
2.3
. We follow mostly the notations used in
[
8
]
, however there are certain changes since we aim to cover the setting for both RSK and dual RSK in this paper. In particular we add either a “
*
” or “dual” to notations used in
[
8
]
whenever these are specific for the dual RSK setting.

For two partitions
\mu,\lambda
we write
\mu\subseteq\lambda
if the Young diagram of
\mu
is contained in the Young diagram of
\lambda
. The poset defined by the inclusion relation
\subseteq
is called
Young’s lattice
; its meet and join are
\cap
and
\cup
, where
\lambda\cap\mu
(resp.,
\lambda\cup\mu
) is defined as the partition obtained by taking the intersection (resp., union) of the corresponding Young diagrams.
For
\mu\subseteq\lambda
the
skew diagram

\lambda/\mu
is defined as the Young diagram obtained by deleting all boxes of
\lambda
which are part of
\mu
. We call the skew diagram
\lambda/\mu
a
horizontal strip
(resp.,
vertical strip
), denoted by
\mu\prec\lambda
(resp.,
\mu\prec^{\prime}\lambda
), if
\lambda/\mu
contains at most one cell in each column (resp. row).
The partitions
\lambda,\mu
are called
interlacing
if
\mu\prec\lambda
.

We denote by
\mathbb{P}
the set of partitions and define
\mathbb{Y}
as the
\mathbb{Q}
-vector space generated by partitions, i.e.,

\mathbb{Y}:=\bigoplus_{\lambda\in\mathbb{P}}\mathbb{Q}\lambda.

Intuitively, this vector space already covers all combinatorial objects we are interested in for this paper. However to be algebraically precise, we need to actually work over the following more general space. For variables
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
we define the
\mathbb{Q}\llbracket x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rrbracket
-module
\mathbb{Y}\llbracket x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rrbracket
as the set of formal power series in the variables
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
with coefficients in
\mathbb{Y}
together with the usual addition and multiplication. For simplicity, we define for the rest of this paper all linear maps over the smallest reasonable field or algebra respectively and extend them linearly in the obvious way if necessary.

The
up operator

U_{x}
, the
down operator

D_{y}
and the
dual down operator

D_{y}^{*}
are
\mathbb{Q}\llbracket x,y\rrbracket
-linear maps on
\mathbb{Y}\llbracket x,y\rrbracket
which are defined as

U_{x}\lambda=\sum_{\nu\succ\lambda}x^{|\nu/\lambda|}\nu,\qquad D_{y}\lambda=\sum_{\mu\prec\lambda}y^{|\lambda/\mu|}\mu,\qquad D_{y}^{*}\lambda=\sum_{\mu\prec^{\prime}\lambda}y^{|\lambda/\mu|}\mu,

for a partition
\lambda
and linearly extended onto
\mathbb{Y}\llbracket x,y\rrbracket
.

A crucial property of these operators are the following commutation relations.

Theorem 2.4
.

The up and (dual) down operator satisfy the commutation relations

(15)

\displaystyle D_{y}U_{x}=\frac{1}{1-xy}U_{x}D_{y},

(16)

\displaystyle D_{y}^{*}U_{x}=(1+xy)U_{x}D_{y}^{*}.

Before proving this theorem, we use it to deduce the (dual) Cauchy identity.
First observe that each SSYT
T
of shape
\lambda
with entries at most
n
can be interpreted as a chain of interlacing partitions

\emptyset=T^{(0)}\prec T^{(1)}\prec\cdots\prec T^{(n)}=\lambda,

where
T^{(i)}
denotes the shape of the tableau
T
when restricted to the entries at most
i
. Analogously a dual SSYT
R
of shape
\lambda
with entries at most
m
can be interpreted as a chain of dual interlacing partitions

\emptyset=R^{(0)}\prec^{\prime}R^{(1)}\prec^{\prime}\cdots\prec^{\prime}R^{(m)}=\lambda.

We define the inner product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
on the vector space
\mathbb{Y}
as
\langle\lambda,\rho\rangle=\delta_{\lambda,\rho}
for all
\lambda,\rho\in\mathbb{P}
. For each
\rho\in\mathbb{P}
we extend the inner product to a linear functional
\left\langle\cdot,\rho\right\rangle
on
\mathbb{Y}\llbracket x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},y_{1},\ldots,y_{m}\rrbracket
linearly. The above observations imply the following alternative formulas for the Schur polynomials
s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}),s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{y})
and
s_{\lambda^{\prime}}(\mathbf{y})

(17)

\displaystyle s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\left\langle U_{x_{n}}\cdots U_{x_{1}}\emptyset,\lambda\right\rangle,

(18)

\displaystyle s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=\left\langle D_{y_{1}}\cdots D_{y_{m}}\lambda,\emptyset\right\rangle,

(19)

\displaystyle s_{\lambda^{\prime}}(\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=\left\langle D_{y_{1}}^{*}\cdots D_{y_{m}}^{*}\lambda,\emptyset\right\rangle,

where
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
and
\mathbf{y}=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{m})
.
We calculate the following linear functional in two different ways; first as shown next

(20)

\left\langle D_{y_{m}}\cdots D_{y_{1}}U_{x_{n}}\cdots U_{x_{1}}\emptyset,\emptyset\right\rangle=\sum_{\lambda}\left\langle U_{x_{n}}\cdots U_{x_{1}}\emptyset,\lambda\right\rangle\left\langle D_{y_{m}}\cdots D_{y_{1}}\lambda,\emptyset\right\rangle=\sum_{\lambda}s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x})s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{y}).

By using the commutation relation (
15
) repeatedly we obtain

D_{y_{j}}U_{x_{n}}\cdots U_{x_{1}}=\frac{1}{1-x_{n}y_{j}}U_{x_{n}}D_{y_{j}}U_{x_{n-1}}\cdots U_{x_{1}}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{1-x_{i}y_{j}}U_{x_{n}}\cdots U_{x_{1}}D_{y_{j}}.

Since
D_{y_{j}}\emptyset=\emptyset
we obtain by using the above equation repeatedly

\left\langle D_{y_{m}}\cdots D_{y_{1}}U_{x_{n}}\cdots U_{x_{1}}\emptyset,\emptyset\right\rangle=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq n\\
1\leq j\leq m\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{1-x_{i}y_{j}}\left\langle U_{x_{n}}\cdots U_{x_{1}}\emptyset,\emptyset\right\rangle=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq n\\
1\leq j\leq m\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{1-x_{i}y_{j}},

which proves together with (
20
) the Cauchy identity (
13
). In order to prove the dual Cauchy identity (
14
) we regard the linear functional

\left\langle D_{y_{m}}^{*}\cdots D_{y_{1}}^{*}U_{x_{n}}\cdots U_{x_{1}}\emptyset,\emptyset\right\rangle

analogously.

In the remainder of this section we provide a combinatorial proof of Theorem
2.4
. For partitions
\lambda,\rho
and a non-negative integer
k
we define the sets

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}(\lambda,\rho,k)

\displaystyle:=\{\nu:\lambda\prec\nu\succ\rho,|\nu/(\lambda\cup\rho)|=k\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(\lambda,\rho,k)

\displaystyle:=\{\mu:\lambda\succ\mu\prec\rho,|(\lambda\cap\rho)/\mu|=k\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}^{*}(\lambda,\rho,k)

\displaystyle:=\{\nu:\lambda\prec^{\prime}\nu\succ\rho,|\nu/(\lambda\cup\rho)|=k\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{*}(\lambda,\rho,k)

\displaystyle:=\{\mu:\lambda\succ\mu\prec^{\prime}\rho,|(\lambda\cap\rho)/\mu|=k\}.

Then (
15
) is equivalent to the system of equations

(21)

\left|\mathcal{U}(\lambda,\rho,k)\right|=\sum_{i=0}^{k}\left|\mathcal{D}(\lambda,\rho,i)\right|,

for all partitions
\lambda,\rho
and non-negative integers
k
, and (
16
) is equivalent to the system of equations

(22)

\left|\mathcal{U}^{*}(\lambda,\rho,k)\right|=\left|\mathcal{D}^{*}(\lambda,\rho,k)\right|+\left|\mathcal{D}^{*}(\lambda,\rho,k-1)\right|,

for all partitions
\lambda,\rho
and non-negative integers
k
.

(a)

The partition
\lambda=(9,7,4,4,4)
together with all maximal horizontal inner ribbons (coloured in red) and all maximal vertical outer ribbons (coloured in blue).

(b)

The partition
\rho=(7,7,5,5,2,1)
together with all maximal vertical inner ribbons (coloured in red) and all maximal horizontal outer ribbons (coloured in blue).

(c)

The partition
\lambda\cap\rho
together with all dual removable inner corners with respect to
(\lambda,\rho)
(coloured in red).

(d)

The partition
\lambda\cup\rho
together with all dual addable outer corners with respect to
(\lambda,\rho)
(coloured in blue).

Figure 1
.
The stepwise construction of the removable inner corners of
\lambda\cap\rho
and addable outer corners of
\lambda\cup\rho
.

In order to prove both systems of equations combinatorially, we need to introduce further notations.

•

A
horizontally inner ribbon
of
\lambda
is set of cells
\{(i,j),(i+1,j),\ldots,(i+k,j)\}=\lambda/\mu
such that
\mu\prec\lambda
.

•

A
vertically inner ribbon
of
\lambda
is set of cells
\{(i,j),(i,j+1),\ldots,(i,j+k)\}=\lambda/\mu
such that
\mu\prec^{\prime}\lambda
.

•

A
horizontally outer ribbon
of
\lambda
is set of cells
\{(i,j),(i+1,j),\ldots,(i+k,j)\}=\nu/\lambda
such that
\lambda\prec\nu
.

•

A
vertically outer ribbon
of
\lambda
is set of cells
\{(i,j),(i,j+1),\ldots,(i,j+k)\}=\nu/\lambda
such that
\lambda\prec^{\prime}\nu
.

Note that the above definitions generalise the notions of inner and outer corners. An
inner corner
is an inner ribbon that is both horizontally and vertically, and analogously an
outer corner
is an outer ribbon that is both horizontally and vertically.
We call a horizontal inner ribbon of
\lambda\cap\rho

removable
with respect to
(\lambda,\rho)
if it is the intersection of a horizontal inner ribbon of
\lambda
and a horizontal inner ribbon of
\rho
. Analogously a horizontal outer ribbon of
\lambda\cup\rho
is called
addable
with respect to
(\lambda,\rho)
if it is the intersection of a horizontal outer ribbon of
\lambda
and a horizontal outer ribbon of
\rho
. For the dual RSK setting, we call an inner corner of
\lambda\cap\rho

dual removable
with respect to
(\lambda,\rho)
if it is the intersection of a horizontal inner ribbon of
\lambda
and a vertical inner ribbon of
\rho
, and analogously we call an outer corner of
\lambda\cup\rho

dual addable
with respect to
(\lambda,\rho)
if it is the intersection of vertical outer ribbon of
\lambda
and a horizontal outer ribbon of
\rho
. See Figure
1
for an example of dual addable and dual removable corners and Figure
2
for an example of addable and removable ribbons. In all of the above settings we omit referring to
(\lambda,\rho)
whenever they are clear from context.

Let
\lambda,\rho
be two partitions and denote by
d
the number of removable ribbons of
\lambda\cap\rho
of size
1
, i.e.
d
is the size of
\mathcal{D}(\lambda,\rho,1)
. We say that a removable ribbon of
\lambda\cap\rho
is
in position
i
if it is in the same row as the
i
-th element of
\mathcal{D}(\lambda,\rho,1)
when counted from bottom starting with
1
. Analogously we say that an addable ribbon of
\lambda\cup\rho
is in position
i
if in the same row as the
i
-th element of
\mathcal{U}(\lambda,\rho,1)
counted from bottom and starting with
0
.

We identify elements of
\mu\in\mathcal{D}(\lambda,\rho,k)
and
\nu\in\mathcal{U}(\lambda,\rho,k)
with multisets
\mathbf{R}(\mu)
of
[d]=\{1,2,\ldots,d\}
or
\mathbf{S}(\nu)
of
[0,d]=\{0,1,\ldots,d\}
respectively as follows.
Given an element
\mu\in\mathcal{D}(\lambda,\rho,k)
we can decompose
(\lambda\cap\rho)/\mu
into a set
\{R_{i_{1}},\ldots,R_{i_{l}}\}
of removable ribbons of
\lambda\cap\rho
such that the
R_{i_{j}}
are pairwise different,
R_{i_{j}}
is in position
i_{j}
, and that

(\lambda\cap\rho)/\mu=\bigcup_{i=1}^{l}R_{i}.

We define the multiset
\mathbf{R}(\mu)
as the multiset that contains the element
i_{j}
with multiplicity
|R_{i_{j}}|
for
1\leq j\leq l
.
The construction is analogue for
\mathbf{S}(\nu)
. We can apply the same constructions for the elements of
\mu^{*}\in\mathcal{D}^{*}(\lambda,\rho,k)
and
\nu^{*}\in\mathcal{U}^{*}(\lambda,\rho,k)
with the only difference that
\mathbf{R}(\mu^{*})
is a
k
-subset of
[d]
and that
\mathbf{S}(\nu^{*})
is a
k
-subset of
[0,d]
.
In the following we construct for each
\lambda,\rho,k
the natural bijections
\F_{\lambda,\rho,k}^{\rm row},\F_{\lambda,\rho,k}^{\rm col},\F_{\lambda,\rho,k}^{{\rm row}*},\F_{\lambda,\rho,k}^{{\rm col}*}
,

\displaystyle\F_{\lambda,\rho,k}^{\bullet}:\bigcup_{i=0}^{k}\mathcal{D}(\lambda,\rho,i)\rightarrow\mathcal{U}(\lambda,\rho,k),\qquad\F_{\lambda,\rho,k}^{\bullet*}:\mathcal{D}^{*}(\lambda,\rho,k)\cup\mathcal{D}^{*}(\lambda,\rho,k-1)\rightarrow\mathcal{U}^{*}(\lambda,\rho,k),

where
\bullet
can be either
{\rm row}
or
{\rm col}
and hence prove Theorem
2.4
bijectively.

(a)

The partition
\lambda\cap\rho
together with all maximal removable ribbons w.r.t
(\lambda,\rho)
.

(b)

The partition
\lambda\cup\rho
together with all maximal addable ribbons w.r.t
(\lambda,\rho)
.

(c)

Schematic of the row insertion map
F^{\rm row}
.

(d)

Schematic of the column insertion map
F^{\rm col}
.

Figure 2
.
The inner and outer horizontal ribbons of
\lambda\cap\rho
or
\lambda\cup\rho
respectively and the schematics of the row and column insertions for
\lambda=(10,5,3,2)
and
\rho=(9,7,3,3)
.

The bijection
\F_{\lambda,\rho,k}^{\rm row}
, called
row insertion
maps a multiset
\mathbf{R}\in\mathcal{D}(\lambda,\rho,j)
to the set
\mathbf{R}\cup\{0^{(k-j)}\}
. The definition of the
column insertion
4
4

4

Note that column insertion is sometimes also called
Burge insertion
, compare for example to
[
23
, §3.2]
.

\F_{\lambda,\rho,k}^{\rm col}
is slightly more complicated. Denote by
\mathbf{S}^{\infty}
the union of all addable ribbons of
\lambda\cup\rho
. In order to obtain
\F_{\lambda,\rho,k}^{\rm col}(\mathbf{R})
we construct a sequence
(A_{i},B_{i})
of pairs of multisets as follows. For
\mathbf{R}\in\mathcal{D}(\lambda,\rho,j)
start with the pair of multisets
(A_{0},B_{0})=(\emptyset,\mathbf{S}^{\infty}\setminus\mathbf{R})
and denote by
x_{1}\leq\cdots\leq x_{k}
the elements of
\mathbf{R}\cup\{\infty^{(k-j)}\}
. In the
i
-th step set
(A_{i},B_{i})=(A_{i-1}\cup\{y\},B_{i-1}\setminus\{y\})
where
y
is the largest element in
B_{i-1}
which is smaller than
x_{i}
. We define
\F_{\lambda,\rho,k}^{\rm col}(\mathbf{R})
as the terminal multiset
A_{k}
. For an alternative description of column insertion see for example
[
23
, §3.2]
. Both maps are illustrated in Figure
2
.

Example 2.5
.

The following table shows the row and column insertion maps for
\lambda=\rho=(3,2)
and
k=2
. The top row contains the elements
\mu\in\bigcup_{i=0}^{2}\mathcal{D}(\lambda,\lambda,i)
, where
\mu
is obtained by removing the red boxes. The two bottom rows contain the corresponding images
\nu\in\mathcal{U}(\lambda,\lambda,2)
where each
\nu
is obtained by adding the blue boxes to
\lambda
.

\F_{\lambda,\rho,k}^{\rm row}

\F_{\lambda,\rho,k}^{\rm col}

For the dual setting the definitions become simpler since we have sets instead of multisets. The bijections
\F_{\lambda,\rho,k}^{{\rm row}*}
, called
dual row insertion
, and
\F_{\lambda,\rho,k}^{{\rm col}*}
, called
dual column insertion
, are defined as

\F_{\lambda,\rho,k}^{{\rm row}*}(\mathbf{R})=\begin{cases}\mathbf{R}\qquad&|\mathbf{R}|=k,\\
\mathbf{R}\cup\{0\}&|\mathbf{R}|=k-1,\end{cases}\qquad\F_{\lambda,\rho,k}^{{\rm col}*}(\mathbf{R})=\begin{cases}\{x-1:x\in\mathbf{R}\}\quad&|\mathbf{R}|=k,\\
\{x-1:x\in\mathbf{R}\}\cup\{d\}&|\mathbf{R}|=k-1,\end{cases}

2.3.
Growth diagrams

In this subsection we define Fomin’s growth diagrams
[
6
,
7
]
which allow us to transform a bijective proof of the commutation relation into a bijective proof of the (dual) Cauchy identity.
In the following we view an
n\times m
matrix as an
n\times m
grid of squares for which we label the vertices of the grid by partitions. The coordinates of the squares lie in
[n]\times[m]
, and the coordinates of the vertices in
[0,n]\times[0,m]
. In both cases these coordinates are interpreted as matrix coordinates instead of Cartesian coordinates.

Definition 2.6
.

(1)

Let
A
be an
n\times m
matrix with non-negative entries. A
growth associated with
A
is an assignment
\Lambda=(\Lambda_{i,j})
of partitions to the vertices
(i,j)\in[0,n]\times[0,m]
such that

•

for
i<n
holds
\Lambda_{i,j}\prec\Lambda_{i+1,j}
, i.e.,
\Lambda_{i+1,j}/\Lambda_{i,j}
is a horizontal strip,

•

for
j<m
holds
\Lambda_{i,j}\prec\Lambda_{i,j+1}
, i.e.,
\Lambda_{i,j+1}/\Lambda_{i,j}
is a horizontal strip,

•

|\Lambda_{i,j}|
is equal to the sum of entries in
A
to the north-west of
(i,j)
, that is,

(23)

|\Lambda_{i,j}|=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq k\leq i\\
1\leq l\leq j\end{subarray}}A_{k,l}.

(2)

Let
B
be an
n\times m

\{0,1\}
-matrix. A
dual growth associated with
B
is an assignment
\Lambda=(\Lambda_{i,j})
of partitions to the vertices
(i,j)\in[0,n]\times[0,m]
such that

•

for
i<n
holds
\Lambda_{i,j}\prec\Lambda_{i+1,j}
, i.e.,
\Lambda_{i+1,j}/\Lambda_{i,j}
is a horizontal strip,

•

for
j<m
holds
\Lambda_{i,j}\prec^{\prime}\Lambda_{i,j+1}
, i.e.,
\Lambda_{i,j+1}/\Lambda_{i,j}
is a vertical strip,

•

|\Lambda_{i,j}|
is equal to the sum of entries in
B
to the north-west of
(i,j)
, that is,

|\Lambda_{i,j}|=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq k\leq i\\
1\leq l\leq j\end{subarray}}B_{k,l}.

Since the matrices
A
or
B
respectively are determined by
\Lambda
we will often omit the reference to it and call
\Lambda
a
growth
or
dual growth
respectively.

\Lambda
we define
P(\Lambda)
as the semistandard Young tableau determined by the last column of
\Lambda
, i.e., the shape of
P(\Lambda)
restricted to entries at most
i
is
\Lambda_{i,m}
, and
Q(\Lambda)
as the (dual) semistandard Young tableau determined by the last row of
\Lambda
, i.e., the shape of
Q(\Lambda)
restricted to entries at most
j
is
\Lambda_{n,j}
. The according tableaux for the third growth in Example

are

If
\Lambda
is a (dual) growth associated with
A
and
P=P(\Lambda),Q=Q(\Lambda)
, we write

\Lambda:A\rightarrow(P,Q),

and say that “
\Lambda
is a (dual) growth from
A
to
(P,Q)
. While in general, there may be multiple (dual) growths from
A
to
(P,Q)
the setting introduced next allows us to choose a unique (dual) growth.

where
k=|\nu/(\lambda\cup\rho)|=|(\lambda\cap\rho)/\mu|+b
.

Definition 2.8
.

A set of
local growth rules

\F_{\bullet}
is a family of bijections

\F_{\lambda,\rho,k}:\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{D}(\lambda,\rho,i)\rightarrow\mathcal{U}(\lambda,\rho,k),

for each ordered pair
\lambda,\rho
and non-negative integer
k
. Analogously, a set of
local dual growth rules

\F_{\bullet}^{*}
is a family of bijections

\F_{\lambda,\rho,k}^{*}:\mathcal{D}^{*}(\lambda,\rho,k)\cup\mathcal{D}^{*}(\lambda,\rho,k-1)\rightarrow\mathcal{U}^{*}(\lambda,\rho,k),

for each ordered pair
\lambda,\rho
and non-negative integer
k
.
A (dual) growth
\Lambda
is called an

\F_{\bullet}
-growth diagram
if each square of
\Lambda
satisfies
\nu=\F_{\lambda,\rho,|(\lambda\cap\rho)/\mu|+a}(\mu)
and an

\F_{\bullet}^{*}
-dual growth diagram
if each square of
\Lambda
satisfies
\nu=\F^{*}_{\lambda,\rho,|(\lambda\cap\rho)/\mu|+b}(\mu)
,
where
\mu,\lambda,\rho,\nu,a,b
are defined as in (
24
) or (
25
) respectively.

The key observation is that each set of local (dual) growth rules yields a bijection between
n\times m
matrices
A
with non-negative entries and pairs
(P,Q)
of SSYT of the same shape, or
n\times m

\{0,1\}
-matrices
B
and pairs
(P,Q)
of same shape, where
P
is an SSYT and
Q
a dual SSYT respectively, and hence a proof of Theorem
2.3
. Indeed, starting with a matrix
A
or
B
, we construct a (dual) growth diagram
\Lambda
using the local (dual) growth rules and then map
\Lambda
to the pair
(P(\Lambda),Q(\Lambda))
. On the other hand, starting with the pair
(P,Q)
of tableaux we can construct a unique (dual) growth diagram by placing the chains of partitions corresponding to
P
or
Q
in the rightmost column or bottommost row and filling the remaining grid (including the matrix entries) according to

\mu=\F_{\lambda,\rho,|\nu/(\lambda\cup\rho)|}^{-1}(\nu),\qquad a=|\nu|+|\mu|-|\lambda|-|\rho|,

for the case of a growth or

\mu={\F_{\lambda,\rho,|\nu/(\lambda\cup\rho)|}^{*}}^{-1}(\nu),\qquad b=|\nu|+|\mu|-|\lambda|-|\rho|,

for the case of a dual growth.

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,.

We denote by
{\rm RSK}_{\F_{\bullet}}
and
{\rm RSK}^{*}_{\F_{\bullet}^{*}}
the bijections
A\mapsto(P,Q)
induced by the local (dual) growth rules
\F_{\bullet}
and
\F_{\bullet}^{*}
as described above. In the remainder of this subsection we describe how
{\rm RSK}_{\F_{\bullet}}
can be translated into an insertion algorithm. The translation of
{\rm RSK}^{*}_{\F_{\bullet}^{*}}
into an insertion algorithm can be done analogously; for a detailed description see
[
8
, Lemma 2.10]
.
Since an insertion algorithm describes how the
P
tableau, also called the
insertion tableau
, changes, it suffices to restrict ourselves to one column of the
\F_{\bullet}
-growth diagram as displayed next. Remember that
T^{(i)}
denotes the shape of a tableau when restricted to entries at most
i
.

T^{(0)}
T^{(1)}
T^{(n-1)}
T^{(n)}
\widehat{T}^{(0)}
\widehat{T}^{(1)}
\widehat{T}^{(n-1)}
\widehat{T}^{(n)}
A_{1,j}
A_{n,j}

Lemma 2.10
.

Given an SSYT
T
and a multiset
I=\{1^{(A_{1,j})},2^{(A_{2,j})},\ldots,n^{(A_{n,j})}\}
which we call the
insertion set
. The tableau
\widehat{T}
as defined in the above partial
\F_{\bullet}
-growth diagram is obtained by the following algorithm.

•

Let
i
be the smallest element in
I
and denote by
k
its multiplicity. Denote by
C
the set of cells of
\F_{\lambda,\rho,k}(\mu)/(\lambda\cup\rho)
where
\mu=T^{(i-1)}
,
\lambda=T^{(i)}
and
\rho=\widehat{T}^{(i-1)}
. Place an entry
i
into all cells of
C
, delete all
i
’s from the insertion set and add all entries to the insertion set which have been bumped, i.e., replaced by an entry
i
.

•

Repeat the previous step until the insertion set is empty.

Proof.

Let
i
be the smallest element in
I
. The positions where an entry
i
of
T
was bumped in a previous step are the cells of
(\lambda\cap\rho)\setminus\mu
, where
\lambda,\rho,\mu
are defined as above. By definition, the multiplicity
k
of
i
in
I
is
A_{i,j}
plus the number of times an entry
i
has been bumped in a previous step, i.e.,
k=A_{i,j}+|(\lambda\cap\rho)\setminus\mu|
. The definition of
\nu=\F_{\lambda,\rho,k}(\mu)
implies
k=|\nu\setminus(\lambda\cup\rho)|
. Hence we can indeed obtain
\nu
by placing all
i
entries of
C
into the shape
\nu\setminus(\lambda\cup\rho)
.
∎

For a set of dual local growth rules
\F^{*}_{\bullet}
there is an analogous algorithm by restricting to sets instead of multisets; compare to
[
8
, Lemma 2.10]
for more details.
We define the

\F_{\bullet}
-insertion
of
I
into
T
as the algorithm described in Lemma
2.10
and denote it by
T\xleftarrow[\F_{\bullet}]{}I
. Analogously we define the
dual
\F^{*}_{\bullet}
-insertion
by restricting the above algorithm to sets.
We call a (dual)
\F_{\bullet}
-insertion
traceable
if

T\xleftarrow[\F_{\bullet}]{}I=T\xleftarrow[\F_{\bullet}]{}\{i_{1}\}\xleftarrow[\F_{\bullet}]{}\{i_{2}\}\xleftarrow[\F_{\bullet}]{}\cdots\xleftarrow[\F_{\bullet}]{}\{i_{k}\},

holds for any SSYT
T
and set
I=\{i_{1}\leq\cdots\leq i_{k}\}
, i.e., we are allowed to insert the elements of
I
“sequentially”. Analogously we call a (dual)
\F_{\bullet}
-insertion
reverse traceable
if

T\xleftarrow[\F_{\bullet}]{}I=T\xleftarrow[\F_{\bullet}]{}\{i_{k}\}\xleftarrow[\F_{\bullet}]{}\{i_{k-1}\}\xleftarrow[\F_{\bullet}]{}\cdots\xleftarrow[\F_{\bullet}]{}\{i_{1}\},

holds for any SSYT
T
and any set
I=\{i_{1}\leq\cdots\leq i_{k}\}
.
It is easy to verify, that the row insertion
\F^{{\rm row}}_{\bullet}
and the dual column insertion
\F^{{\rm col}^{*}}_{\bullet}
are traceable and that the column insertion
\F^{{\rm col}}_{\bullet}
and the dual row insertion
\F^{{\rm row}^{*}}_{\bullet}
are reverse traceable; compare for example with
[
23
, Section 3]
.

Given an
n\times m
matrix
A
with non-negative entries, we can construct the image of
{\rm RSK}_{\F_{\bullet}}
using the
\F_{\bullet}
-insertion as follows. First we construct the biword
\omega_{A}
by reading the columns of
A
from top to bottom, starting with the leftmost column, and appending
A_{i,j}
-times
\begin{pmatrix}j\\
i\end{pmatrix}
to
\omega_{A}
.
Given a biword

\omega_{A}=\begin{pmatrix}j_{1}&\cdots&j_{1}&\cdots&\cdots&j_{k}&\cdots&j_{k}\\
i_{1,1}&\cdots&i_{1,l_{1}}&\cdots&\cdots&i_{k,1}&\cdots&i_{k,l_{k}}\end{pmatrix},

the
insertion tableau

P
is defined as

P=\emptyset\xleftarrow[\F_{\bullet}]{}\{i_{1,1},\ldots,i_{1,l_{1}}\}\xleftarrow[\F_{\bullet}]{}\cdots\xleftarrow[\F_{\bullet}]{}\{i_{k,1},\ldots,i_{k,l_{k}}\}.

The
recording tableau

Q
is defined as the SSYT which records the growth of
P
by placing
j_{r}
into the cells which were added during the
\F_{\bullet}
-insertion of
\{i_{r,1},\ldots,i_{r,l_{r}}\}
.

Example 2.11
.

The biword
\omega_{A}
of the matrix

A=\begin{pmatrix}0&2&1\\
1&1&0\\
2&0&0\end{pmatrix}

as in Example
2.7
is

\omega_{A}=\begin{pmatrix}1&1&1&2&2&2&3\\
2&3&3&1&1&2&1\end{pmatrix}.

Using that
\F^{\rm row}_{\bullet}
is traceable, the stepwise construction of the insertion tableau
P
and the recording tableau
Q
of
{\rm RSK}_{\F_{\bullet}^{\rm row}}
is shown next.

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#FFFFFF} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.3pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@fill@opacity{1}\lxSVG@begingroup@{fill-opacity=1} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 39.37 M 0 39.37 L 0 59.06 L 19.69 59.06 L 19.69 39.37 Z M 19.69 59.06}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}&=Q\end{array}

By comparing with Example
2.7
, we see that we obtain the same pair of tableaux.

Remark 2.12
.

We call a set of local growth rules
\F_{\bullet}

symmetric
, if
\F_{\lambda,\rho,k}(\mu)=\F_{\rho,\lambda,k}(\mu)
for all partitions
\lambda,\rho,\mu
and integers
k
. It is immediate that the
{\rm RSK}_{\F_{\bullet}}
correspondence of a symmetric set of local growth rules
\F_{\bullet}
satisfies the following property

(26)

{\rm RSK}_{\F_{\bullet}}(A)=(P,Q)\qquad\Leftrightarrow\qquad{\rm RSK}_{\F_{\bullet}}(A^{T})=(Q,P).

It is not difficult to see that the sets
\mathcal{D}(\lambda,\rho,k)
and
\mathcal{U}(\lambda,\rho,k)
can be expressed by using
\lambda\cap\rho
and
\lambda\cup\rho
instead of
\lambda,\rho
. Hence all local growth rules whose definition is based on addable and removable ribbons, in particular
\F_{\bullet}^{{\rm row}}
and
\F_{\bullet}^{\rm col}
, are symmetric.

2.4.
Skew identities

One of the advantages of using growth diagrams is that we can generalise the above results immediately to the setting of
skew shapes
without any extra work.

Let
\mu\subseteq\lambda
be two partitions. A filling of the cells of
\lambda/\mu
with positive integers is called

•

a
semistandard Young tableau (SSYT)
of skew shape
\lambda/\mu
if the entries are weakly increasing along rows and strictly increasing along columns,

•

a
dual semistandard Young tableau
of skew shape
\lambda/\mu
if the entries are strictly increasing along rows and weakly increasing along columns.

We denote by
{\rm SSYT}_{\lambda/\mu}(n)
the set of semistandard Young tableaux of skew shape
\lambda/\mu
with entries at most
n
and by
{\rm SSYT}_{\lambda/\mu}^{*}(n)
the set of dual semistandard Young tableaux of skew shape
\lambda/\mu
and entries at most
n
. The
Schur polynomial

s_{\lambda/\mu}(\mathbf{x})
is defined for a family of variables
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
as

s_{\lambda/\mu}(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{T\in{\rm SSYT}_{\lambda/\mu}(n)}\mathbf{x}^{T},

where the weight
\mathbf{x}^{T}
of a skew shaped tableau
T
is defined analogously to the regular case.

Theorem 2.13
.

Let
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
and
\mathbf{y}=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{m})
be two sequences of variables and
\lambda,\rho
be two partitions. Then

(27)

\displaystyle\sum_{\nu}s_{\nu/\rho}(\mathbf{x})s_{\nu/\lambda}(\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq n\\
1\leq j\leq m\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{1-x_{i}y_{j}}\sum_{\mu}s_{\lambda/\mu}(\mathbf{x})s_{\rho/\mu}(\mathbf{y}),

(28)

\displaystyle\sum_{\nu}s_{\nu/\rho}(\mathbf{x})s_{\nu^{\prime}/\lambda^{\prime}}(\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq n\\
1\leq j\leq m\end{subarray}}(1+x_{i}y_{j})\sum_{\mu}s_{\lambda/\mu}(\mathbf{x})s_{\rho^{\prime}/\mu^{\prime}}(\mathbf{y}).

Algebraically, both identities follow immediately from the commutation relations in Theorem
2.4
as described in the paragraph after (
20
) by using the fact, that the Schur polynomials for skew shapes can be expressed as

(29)

\displaystyle s_{\lambda/\mu}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\left\langle U_{x_{n}}\cdots U_{x_{1}}\mu,\lambda\right\rangle,

(30)

\displaystyle s_{\lambda/\mu}(\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=\left\langle D_{y_{1}}\cdots D_{y_{m}}\lambda,\mu\right\rangle,

(31)

\displaystyle s_{\lambda^{\prime}/\mu^{\prime}}(\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=\left\langle D_{y_{1}}^{*}\cdots D_{y_{m}}^{*}\lambda,\mu\right\rangle.

On the other side, the skew Cauchy identities in Theorem
2.13
actually imply the commutation relations in Theorem
2.4
, i.e., both theorems are equivalent to each other. Indeed, by restricting to one variable in both families, i.e.,
\mathbf{x}=(x),\mathbf{y}=(y)
, the left hand side of (
27
) is equal to

\sum_{\nu}s_{\nu/\rho}(x)s_{\nu/\lambda}(y)=\sum_{\nu}\left\langle U_{x}\rho,\nu\right\rangle\left\langle D_{y}\nu,\lambda\right\rangle=\left\langle D_{y}U_{x}\rho,\lambda\right\rangle,

and the right hand side of (
27
) is equal to

\frac{1}{1-xy}\sum_{\mu}s_{\lambda/\mu}(x)s_{\rho/\mu}(y)=\frac{1}{1-xy}\sum_{\mu}\left\langle U_{x}\mu,\lambda\right\rangle\left\langle D_{y}\rho,\mu\right\rangle=\left\langle\frac{1}{1-xy}U_{x}D_{y}\rho,\lambda\right\rangle,

which implies (
15
). The commutation relation (
16
) follows analogously from (
28
).

The immediate generalisation of the algebraic proof to the skew shape setting can also be transferred to the combinatorial level. We present this explicitly for (
27
), the case (
28
) is obtained analogously. First we interpret the left hand side of (
27
) as the generating function of pairs
(P,Q)
of semistandard Young tableaux where
P\in{\rm SSYT}_{\nu/\rho}(n)
and
Q\in{\rm SSYT}_{\nu/\lambda}(m)
with respect to the weight
\mathbf{x}^{P}\mathbf{y}^{Q}
, and the right hand side as the generating function of triples
(A,S,T)
where
A
is an
n\times m
matrix with non-negative integer entries,
S\in{\rm SSYT}_{\lambda/\mu}(n)
, and
T\in{\rm SSYT}_{\rho/\mu}(m)
with respect to the weight

\mathbf{x}^{S}\mathbf{y}^{T}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq n\\
1\leq j\leq m\end{subarray}}(x_{i}y_{j})^{a_{i,j}}.

For a given set of local growth rules
\F_{\bullet}
we can easily extend
{\rm RSK}_{\F_{\bullet}}
to a weight preserving bijection between such triples
(A,S,T)
and pairs
(P,Q)
. For a triple
(A,S,T)
, we construct an
\F_{\bullet}
-growth diagram
\Lambda
associated to
(A,S,T)
by setting
\Lambda_{i,0}=S^{(i)}
,
\Lambda_{0,j}=T^{(j)}
and using the local growth rules
\F_{\bullet}
to fill the rest of
\Lambda
. We define
P=P(\Lambda)
and
Q=Q(\Lambda)
as before.
Note that the resulting growth
\Lambda
is actually a generalisation of the objects defined in Definition
2.6
by replacing the condition (
23
) with

|\Lambda_{i,j}|=|S^{(i)}|+|T^{(j)}|-|S^{(0)}|+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq k\leq i\\
1\leq l\leq j\end{subarray}}A_{k,l}.

For simplicity we refer to both objects as
growths
.
Since the local growth rules
F_{\bullet}
are bijections, it is immediate that
{\rm RSK}_{\F_{\bullet}}
proves (
27
) bijectively.

As another consequence we obtain combinatorial proofs for the (dual) Pieri rules. Indeed, the skew (dual) Cauchy identity for
\rho=\mu=\emptyset
and
\mathbf{y}=(y_{1})
implies

(32)

\displaystyle\sum_{k\geq 0}y_{1}^{k}h_{k}(\mathbf{x})s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x})=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{1-x_{i}y_{1}}s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{\nu}s_{\nu}(\mathbf{x})s_{\nu/\lambda}(y_{1})=\sum_{\nu\succ\lambda}y_{1}^{|\nu/\lambda|}s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}),

(33)

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{n}y_{1}^{k}e_{k}(\mathbf{x})s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x})=\prod_{i=1}^{n}(1+x_{i}y_{1})s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{\nu}s_{\nu}(\mathbf{x})s_{\nu^{\prime}/\lambda^{\prime}}(y_{1})=\sum_{\nu\succ^{\prime}\lambda}y_{1}^{|\nu/\lambda|}s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x}),

where we used the generating functions for
h_{k}(\mathbf{x})
and
e_{k}(\mathbf{x})
on the one hand and the fact that
s_{\nu/\lambda}(y_{1})
is
0
unless
\nu/\lambda
is a horizontal strip. By comparing the coefficient of
y_{1}^{k}
on both sides of (
32
) and (
33
) respectively, we obtain the Pieri and dual Pieri rule

(34)

h_{k}(\mathbf{x})s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu\succ\lambda\\
|\nu/\lambda|=k\end{subarray}}s_{\nu}(\lambda),\qquad e_{k}(\mathbf{x})s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\nu\succ^{\prime}\lambda\\
|\nu/\lambda|=k\end{subarray}}s_{\nu}(\lambda).

For each choice of local (dual) growth rules
\F_{\bullet}
we obtain a bijection between all pairs
(T,(a_{i})_{i})
where
T\in{\rm SSYT}_{\lambda}(n)
and
(a_{i})_{1\leq i\leq n}
is a sequence of non-negative integers (resp.,
a_{i}\in\{0,1\}
) with sum equal to
k
and all
\widehat{T}\in{\rm SSYT}_{\nu}(n)
where
\nu\succ\lambda
(resp.,
\nu\succ^{\prime}\lambda
) and
|\nu/\lambda|=k
by
\F_{\bullet}
-inserting (resp.,
\F_{\bullet}^{*}
-inserting)
\{1^{(a_{1})},\ldots,n^{(a_{n})}\}
into
T
.

3.
Classical Littlewood identities

3.1.
Projection identities

In this section we introduce a new type of identity relating the up and (dual) down operators which we call
projection identities
. While the commutation relations of the previous section allow us to interchange the operators, these new identities allow us to “replace” up operators by (dual) down operators.

For a set
X
of partitions we define the
\mathbb{Q}
-linear map
\pi_{X}:\mathbb{Y}\rightarrow\mathbb{Q}
as

\pi_{X}(\mu):=\sum_{\lambda\in X}\left\langle\mu,\lambda\right\rangle.

Note that the linear extension of
\pi_{X}
to
\mathbb{Y}\llbracket x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rrbracket
is well-defined even for an infinite set
X
since the coefficients of the monomials of an element in
\mathbb{Y}\llbracket x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\rrbracket
are always finite formal sums of partitions.
Following the convention of Ayyer and Kumari
[
1
, Definition 2.9]
we call a partition

k
-asymmetric
if it has Frobenius notation
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{l}|a_{1}+k,\ldots,a_{l}+k)
for a non-negative integer
k
and
(a_{1}-k,\ldots,a_{l}-k|a_{1},\ldots,a_{l})
for a negative integer
k
. Denote by
\mathbb{P}_{e}
the set of partitions with even parts,
\mathbb{P}_{e}^{\prime}
the set of partitions with even columns, and by
\mathbb{P}_{{\rm asym}}^{k}
the set of
k
-asymmetric partitions.

Theorem 3.1
.

The up and (dual) down operators satisfy the following
projection identities

(35)

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathbb{P}}U_{x}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-x}\pi_{\mathbb{P}}D_{x},

(36)

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathbb{P}_{e}}U_{x}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-x^{2}}\pi_{\mathbb{P}_{e}}D_{x},

(37)

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathbb{P}_{e}^{\prime}}U_{x}

\displaystyle=\pi_{\mathbb{P}_{e}^{\prime}}D_{x},

(38)

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathbb{P}_{\rm asym}^{1}}U_{x}

\displaystyle=\pi_{\mathbb{P}_{\rm asym}^{1}}D_{x}^{*},

(39)

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathbb{P}_{\rm asym}^{-1}}U_{x}

\displaystyle=(1+x^{2})\pi_{\mathbb{P}_{\rm asym}^{-1}}D_{x}^{*}.

Before proving the above theorem we show how it yields an algebraic proof of the classical Littlewood identity (
5
) for
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
. The other Littlewood identities follow analogously.
Using (
17
), we can rewrite the left hand side of (
5
) as

\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{P}}s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbb{P}}\left\langle U_{x_{n}}\cdots U_{x_{1}}\emptyset,\lambda\right\rangle=\pi_{\mathbb{P}}U_{x_{n}}\cdots U_{x_{1}}\emptyset.

By the commutation relation (
15
) and the projection identity (
35
) we can further rewrite the above as

\pi_{\mathbb{P}}U_{x_{n}}\cdots U_{x_{1}}\emptyset=\frac{1}{1-x_{n}}\pi_{\mathbb{P}}D_{x_{n}}U_{x_{m-1}}\cdots U_{x_{1}}\emptyset\\
=\frac{1}{1-x_{n}}\cdot\frac{1}{1-x_{n-1}x_{n}}\cdot\pi_{\mathbb{P}}U_{x_{n-1}}D_{x_{n}}U_{x_{n-2}}\cdots U_{x_{1}}\emptyset=\cdots\\
=\frac{1}{1-x_{n}}\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}\frac{1}{1-x_{i}x_{n}}\cdot\pi_{\mathbb{P}}U_{x_{n-1}}\cdots U_{x_{1}}D_{x_{n}}\emptyset\\
=\frac{1}{1-x_{n}}\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}\frac{1}{1-x_{i}x_{n}}\cdot\pi_{\mathbb{P}}U_{x_{n-1}}\cdots U_{x_{1}}\emptyset=\cdots=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{1-x_{i}}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\frac{1}{1-x_{i}x_{j}},

where we used
D_{x}\emptyset=\emptyset
.

As for the commutation relations, we prove Theorem
3.1
bijectively. In particular we relate the equations (
35
) and (
36
) to special cases of (
15
), and provide explicit bijections for the equations (
38
) and (
39
). The equation (
37
) on the other hand turns out to be “trivial”.
For a partition
\lambda
, an integer
k
and a subset
X
of partitions we define the sets

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}_{X}(\lambda,k)

\displaystyle:=\{\nu\in X:\nu\succ\lambda,|\nu/\lambda|=k\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{X}(\lambda,k)

\displaystyle:=\{\mu\in X:\mu\prec\lambda,|\lambda/\mu|=k\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{X}^{*}(\lambda,k)

\displaystyle:=\{\mu\in X:\mu\prec^{\prime}\lambda,|\lambda/\mu|=k\}.

The projection identities (
35
), (
36
), and (
37
) are equivalent to the respective system of equations

\left|\mathcal{U}_{\mathbb{P}}(\lambda,k)\right|=\sum_{i=0}^{k}\left|\mathcal{D}_{\mathbb{P}}(\lambda,i)\right|,\qquad\left|\mathcal{U}_{\mathbb{P}_{e}}(\lambda,k)\right|=\sum_{i=0}^{\left\lfloor\frac{k}{2}\right\rfloor}\left|\mathcal{D}_{\mathbb{P}_{e}}(\lambda,k-2i)\right|,\qquad\left|\mathcal{U}_{\mathbb{P}_{e}^{\prime}}(\lambda,k)\right|=\left|\mathcal{D}_{\mathbb{P}_{e}^{\prime}}(\lambda,k)\right|,

and the identities (
38
) and (
39
) are equivalent to the respective system of equations

\left|\mathcal{U}_{\mathbb{P}_{\rm asym}^{1}}(\lambda,k)\right|=\left|\mathcal{D}_{\mathbb{P}_{\rm asym}^{1}}(\lambda,k)\right|,\qquad\qquad\left|\mathcal{U}_{\mathbb{P}_{\rm asym}^{-1}}(\lambda,k)\right|=\left|\mathcal{D}_{\mathbb{P}_{\rm asym}^{-1}}(\lambda,k)\right|+\left|\mathcal{D}_{\mathbb{P}_{\rm asym}^{-1}}(\lambda,k-2)\right|,

for all partitions
\lambda
and non-negative integers
k
.

3.1.1.
Proof of (
35
)

Let
\F_{\bullet}
be a set of local growth rules. It is immediate that
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbb{P}}(\lambda,k)=\mathcal{D}(\lambda,\lambda,k)
and
\mathcal{U}_{\mathbb{P}}(\lambda,k)=\mathcal{U}(\lambda,\lambda,k)
holds for all partitions
\lambda
and integers
k
. Hence the map
\F_{\mathbb{P},\lambda,k}

(40)

\F_{\mathbb{P},\lambda,k}:\bigcup_{i\leq k}\mathcal{D}_{\mathbb{P}}(\lambda,i)\rightarrow\mathcal{U}_{\mathbb{P}}(\lambda,k):\quad\mu\mapsto\F_{\lambda,\lambda,k}(\mu).

is well defined and a bijection, which yields a bijective proof of (
35
).

3.1.2.
Proof of (
36
)

For a partition
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n})
define the partitions
\lambda^{-},\lambda^{+}
as

\lambda^{-}=\left(\left\lfloor\frac{\lambda_{1}}{2}\right\rfloor,\ldots,\left\lfloor\frac{\lambda_{n}}{2}\right\rfloor\right),\qquad\lambda^{+}=\left(\left\lceil\frac{\lambda_{1}}{2}\right\rceil,\ldots,\left\lceil\frac{\lambda_{n}}{2}\right\rceil\right),

and write
{\rm odd}(\lambda)
for the number of odd parts of
\lambda
.
One can verify easily that the maps

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(\lambda^{-},\lambda^{+},k)\rightarrow\mathcal{D}_{\mathbb{P}_{e}}(\lambda,2k+{\rm odd}(\lambda)):\quad(\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{n})\mapsto(2\mu_{1},\ldots,2\mu_{n}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}(\lambda^{-},\lambda^{+},k)\rightarrow\mathcal{U}_{\mathbb{P}_{e}}(\lambda,2k+{\rm odd}(\lambda)):\quad(\nu_{1},\ldots,\nu_{n})\mapsto(2\nu_{1},\ldots,2\nu_{n}),

are bijections; compare with Figure
3
. By abuse of notation, we call both bijections
\varphi
.
For a given set of local growth rules
\F_{\bullet}
, we define the map
\F_{\mathbb{P}_{e},\lambda,2k+{\rm odd}(\lambda)}
as

(41)

\F_{\mathbb{P}_{e},\lambda,2k+{\rm odd}(\lambda)}=\varphi\circ\F_{\lambda^{-},\lambda^{+},k}\circ\varphi^{-1}.

It is immediate by the above considerations, that
\F_{\mathbb{P}_{e},\lambda,2k+{\rm odd}(\lambda)}
is a bijection between the sets
\bigcup_{i\leq k}\mathcal{D}_{\mathbb{P}_{e}}(\lambda,2i+{\rm odd}(\lambda))
and
\mathcal{U}_{\mathbb{P}_{e}}(\lambda,2k+{\rm odd}(\lambda))
and hence yields a bijective proof of (
36
).

Figure 3
.
On the top row are the partitions
\lambda^{-}
and
\lambda^{+}
for
\lambda=(11,6,3)
, where the boxes lying in an inner (resp., outer) horizontal ribbon are marked by dots. The bottom row contains the corresponding images under
\varphi
, where the boxes which have to be removed (resp. added) to obtain
\lambda
are filled in red (resp. blue) and single dots are mapped to pairs of dots.

3.1.3.
Proof of (
37
)

Let
\lambda
be a partition. Then both sets
\mathcal{U}_{\mathbb{P}_{e}^{\prime}}(\lambda,k)
and
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbb{P}_{e}^{\prime}}(\lambda,k)
are empty unless
k={\rm odd}(\lambda^{\prime})
in which case
\mathcal{U}_{\mathbb{P}_{e}^{\prime}}(\lambda,k)
consists of the partition
\nu
obtained from
\lambda
by adding to each odd column a cell, and
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbb{P}_{e}^{\prime}}(\lambda,k)
consists of the partition
\mu
obtained from
\lambda
by removing a cell of each odd column. We denote by
\F_{\mathbb{P}_{e}^{\prime},\lambda,{\rm odd}(k)}
the bijection from
\{\mu\}
to
\{\nu\}
.

3.1.4.
Proof of (
38
)

2

Figure 4
.
On the left a sketch of all
\mu\in\mathbb{P}^{1}_{{\rm asym}}
with
\mu\prec^{\prime}\lambda=(7,5,3,2,0|10,7,4,2,1)
, where the boxes along the diagonal are marked gray, the boxes which have to be removed are in red and a box labelled
i
is removed if
i\in\mathbf{R}^{1}_{\lambda}(\mu)
.
On the right a sketch of all
\nu\in\mathbb{P}^{1}_{{\rm asym}}
with
\nu\succ\lambda
, where boxes are in blue if they have to be added and a label
i
marks a box which is added if
i\in\mathbf{S}^{1}_{\lambda}(\nu)
.

Let
\lambda\prec\nu
be two partitions with Frobenius notations
\lambda=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{l}|b_{1},\ldots,b_{l})
and
\nu=(c_{1},\ldots,c_{m}|c_{1}+1,\ldots,c_{m}+1)
, i.e.,
\nu\in\mathbb{P}^{1}_{{\rm asym}}
. First observe that
l=m
holds, otherwise we would need to add at least two cells to
\lambda
in column
l+1
which contradicts
\lambda\prec\nu
. The condition
\lambda\prec\nu
is equivalent to the two families of inequalities

c_{i}\geq a_{i}\geq c_{i+1}+1\qquad c_{i}+1\geq b_{i}\geq c_{i},,

for all
1\leq i\leq l
where we set
c_{l+1}=-1
. Hence the existence of the partition
\nu
implies

(42)

b_{1}\geq a_{1}\geq b_{2}\geq\cdots\geq b_{l}\geq a_{l}\qquad\Leftrightarrow\qquad(a_{1},\ldots,a_{l})\prec(b_{1},\ldots,b_{l}).

Since both
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{l})
and
(b_{1},\ldots,b_{l})
are strict partitions, (
42
) is actually equivalent to the existence of a partition
\nu
as above.
Define
\mathbf{S}^{1}_{\lambda}:=\{i:a_{i-1}>b_{i}>a_{i}\}
, where we set
a_{0}=\infty
. Assume that (
42
) holds, then each
\nu\in\mathbb{P}^{1}_{{\rm asym}}
with
\lambda\prec\nu
corresponds to a subset of
\mathbf{S}_{\lambda}^{1}
as follows. First, note that
b_{i}=a_{i}
implies
c_{i}=b_{i}
, and
b_{i}=a_{i-1}
implies
c_{i}=b_{i}-1
for all
i
, compare with Figure
4
. On the other hand for
i\in\mathbf{S}_{\lambda}^{1}
we can have either
c_{i}=b_{i}-1
or
c_{i}=b_{i}
. We define
\mathbf{S}^{1}_{\lambda}(\nu)
as the set of
i\in\mathbf{S}_{\lambda}^{1}
such that
c_{i}=b_{i}
. It is immediate, that
\nu\mapsto\mathbf{S}^{1}_{\lambda}(\nu)
is a bijection between all
\nu
of the above form and all subsets of
\mathbf{S}_{\lambda}^{1}
.

Let
\mu\prec^{\prime}\lambda
where
\mu
has Frobenius notation
(d_{1},\ldots,d_{m}|d_{1}+1,\ldots,d_{m}+1)
. Observe that this implies
m\in\{l,l-1\}
. In the case
m=l-1
it is convenient to define
d_{l}=-1
. The condition
\mu\prec^{\prime}\lambda
is equivalent to the family of inequalities

d_{i}+1\geq a_{i}\geq d_{i},\qquad d_{i-1}\geq b_{i}\geq d_{i}+1,

where we set
d_{0}=\infty
. As before, the existence of
\mu
is therefore equivalent to (
42
). Denote by
\mathbf{R}_{\lambda}^{1}:=\{i:b_{i+1}<a_{i}<b_{i}\}
, where we set
b_{l+1}=-1
. Each
\mu\in\mathbb{P}^{1}_{{\rm asym}}
with
\mu\prec^{\prime}\lambda
corresponds exactly to one subset of
\mathbf{R}_{\lambda}^{1}
as shown next. First,
a_{i}=b_{i}
implies
d_{i}=a_{i}-1
, and
a_{i}=b_{i+1}
implies
d_{i}=a_{i}
. On the other hand for
i\in\mathbf{R}_{\lambda}^{1}
we have the two choices
d_{i}=a_{i}
or
d_{i}=a_{i}-1
. Denote by
\mathbf{R}_{\lambda}^{1}(\mu)
the set of all
i\in\mathbf{R}_{\lambda}^{1}
such that
d_{i}=a_{i}-1
. It is easy to see that the map
\mu\mapsto\mathbf{R}^{1}_{\lambda}(\mu)
is a bijection between all
\mu
of the above form and all subsets of
\mathbf{R}_{\lambda}^{1}
.

It is not difficult to see, for example by comparing with Figure
4
, that
|\nu/\lambda|=|\lambda/\mu|
if
|\mathbf{S}^{1}_{\lambda}(\nu)|=|\mathbf{R}^{1}_{\lambda}(\mu)|
. Hence it suffices to show that
|\mathbf{S}^{1}_{\lambda}|=|\mathbf{R}^{1}_{\lambda}|
in order to prove (
38
).
We place the sequence
b_{1},a_{1},b_{2},a_{2},\ldots
in a zigzag pattern and connect two integers if they are equal. For
(a_{i})_{i}=(7,5,3,2,0)
and
(b_{i})_{i}=(10,7,4,2,1)
this is shown next.
10
7
4
2
1
7
5
3
2
0

Since
\mathbf{R}_{\lambda}^{1}
corresponds to the numbers in the top row which are not connected, and
\mathbf{S}_{\lambda}^{1}
corresponds to the numbers in the bottom row which are not connected, it is immediate that
|\mathbf{S}_{\lambda}^{1}|=|\mathbf{R}_{\lambda}^{1}|
.

We define a natural bijection between the sets
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbb{P}_{{\rm asym}}^{1}}(\lambda,k)
and
\mathcal{U}_{\mathbb{P}_{{\rm asym}}^{1}}(\lambda,k)
denoted by
\F_{\mathbb{P}_{{\rm asym}}^{1},\lambda,k}^{{\rm row}*}
.
For
\mathbf{R}^{1}_{\lambda}=\{r_{1},\ldots,r_{n}\}
and
\mathbf{S}^{1}_{\lambda}=\{s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}\}
we define
\F_{\mathbb{P}_{{\rm asym}}^{1},\lambda,2k}^{{\rm row}*}
to map the partition
\mu
with
\mathbf{R}_{\lambda}^{1}(\mu)=\{r_{i_{1}},\ldots,r_{i_{k}}\}
to the partition
\nu
with
\mathbf{S}_{\lambda}^{1}(\nu)=\{s_{i_{1}},\ldots,s_{i_{k}}\}
.

3.1.5.
Proof of (
39
)

2
3
3
2
1
3

5
3
1

Figure 5
.

On the left a sketch of all
\mu\in\mathbb{P}^{-1}_{{\rm asym}}
with
\mu\prec^{\prime}\lambda=(7,5,3,2|8,5,2,0)
, where the boxes along the diagonal are marked gray, the boxes which have to be removed are in red and a box labelled
i
is removed if
i\in\mathbf{R}^{-1}_{\lambda}(\mu)
.
On the right a sketch of all
\nu\in\mathbb{P}^{-1}_{{\rm asym}}
with
\nu\succ\lambda
, where boxes are in blue if they have to be added and a label
i
marks a box which is added if
i\in\mathbf{S}^{-1}_{\lambda}(\nu)
.

Let
\lambda=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{l}|b_{1},\ldots,b_{l})
and
\nu=(c_{1}+1,\ldots,c_{m}+1|c_{1},\ldots,c_{m})\in\mathbb{P}_{\rm asym}^{-1}
such that
\lambda\prec\nu
. First observe that we have
m\in\{l,l+1\}
and for convenience define
c_{l+1}=-\infty
if
m=l
.
By definition, the condition
\lambda\prec\nu
is equivalent to the two families of inequalities

c_{i}+1\geq a_{i}\geq c_{i+1}+2,\qquad c_{i}\geq b_{i}\geq c_{i}-1,

for all
1\leq i\leq l
. Hence the existence of a partition
\nu
as above is equivalent to

(43)

b_{1}+2\geq a_{1}\geq b_{2}+2\geq\cdots\geq b_{l}+2\geq a_{l}\qquad\Leftrightarrow\qquad(a_{1},\ldots,a_{l})\prec(b_{1}+2,\ldots,b_{l}+2),

where the equivalence holds since
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{l})
and
(b_{1},\ldots,b_{l})
are strict partitions.
Define
\mathbf{S}_{\lambda}^{-1}=\{i\leq l+1:a_{i-1}>b_{i}+2>a_{i}\}
, where we set
a_{0}=\infty
,
b_{l+1}=-1
and
a_{l+1}=-\infty
. We show that each
\nu
with
\lambda\prec\nu
corresponds to a subset of
\mathbf{S}_{\lambda}^{-1}
as follows. First note that
b_{i}+2=a_{i}
implies
c_{i}=b_{i}+1
, and
b_{i}+2=a_{i-1}
implies
c_{i}=b_{i}
. On the other hand if
a_{i-1}>b_{i}+2>a_{i}
holds, we have two allowed choices for
c_{i}
, namely
b_{i}
and
b_{i}+1
. In particular we can have
c_{l+1}=1
if and only if
a_{l}\geq 2
which is equivalent to
(l+1)\in\mathbf{S}_{\lambda}^{-1}
. We define
\mathbf{S}_{\lambda}^{-1}(\nu)
as the set of
i\in\mathbf{S}_{\lambda}^{-1}
such that
c_{i}=b_{i}+1
. By the above argument it is immediate that
\nu\mapsto\mathbf{S}_{\lambda}^{-1}(\nu)
is a bijection between all
\nu
as above and all subsets of
\mathbf{S}_{\lambda}^{-1}
.

Let
\mu=(d_{1}+1,\ldots,d_{m}+1|d_{1},\ldots,d_{m})
be a partition with
\mu\prec^{\prime}\lambda
. The existence of such a
\mu
is equivalent to the family of inequalities

d_{i}+2\geq a_{i}\geq d_{i}+1,\qquad d_{i-1}-1\geq b_{i}\geq d_{i},

which is again equivalent to (
43
). Denote by
\mathbf{R}_{\lambda}^{-1}:=\{i\leq l:b_{i}+2>a_{i}>b_{i+1}+2\}
. Each
\mu\in\mathbb{P}_{{\rm asym}}^{-1}
with
\mu\prec^{\prime}\lambda
corresponds to a subset of
\mathbf{R}_{\lambda}^{-1}
as follows. First, the equality
a_{i}=b_{i}+2
implies
d_{i}=a_{i}-2
, and
a_{i}=b_{i+1}+2
implies
d_{i}=a_{i}-1
. Note that the case
a_{i}<b_{i+1}+2
can only appear for
i=l
and
a_{l}=0
which implies
m=l-1
. For all
i\in\mathbf{R}_{\lambda}^{-1}
we have however two possible choices for
d_{i}
, namely
a_{i}-1
or
a_{i}-2
. Denote by
\mathbf{R}_{\lambda}^{-1}(\mu)
the set of all
i\in\mathbf{R}_{\lambda}^{-1}
such that
d_{i}=a_{i}-2
. Then the above shows that the map
\mu\mapsto\mathbf{R}_{\lambda}^{-1}(\mu)
is a bijection between all such partitions
\mu
and all subsets of
\mathbf{R}_{\lambda}^{-1}
.

Denote by
\nu_{0}
the partition such that
\mathbf{S}_{\lambda}^{-1}(\nu_{0})=\emptyset
. Then we have

|\nu/\lambda|=|\nu_{0}/\lambda|+2|\mathbf{S}_{\lambda}^{-1}(\nu)|,\qquad|\lambda/\mu|=|\nu_{0}/\lambda|+2|\mathbf{R}_{\lambda}^{-1}(\mu)|.

Hence in order to prove (
39
) it suffices to show
|\mathbf{S}_{\lambda}^{-1}|=|\mathbf{R}_{\lambda}^{-1}|+1
.
Similar to the previous subsection we place the sequence
b_{1}+2,a_{1},b_{2}+2,a_{2},\ldots,b_{l}+2,a_{l},b_{l+1}+2
in a zigzag pattern and connect two integers if they are equal and connect
a_{l}
and
b_{l+1}+2
if
a_{l}\leq b_{l+1}+2=1
. For
(a_{i})_{i}=(7,5,3,2)
and
(b_{i})_{i}=(8,5,2,0,-1)
this is shown next.
10
7

2

Again the unconnected numbers in the top row correspond to the elements in
\mathbf{R}_{\lambda}^{-1}
and the unconnected numbers in the bottom row correspond to the elements of
\mathbf{S}_{\lambda}^{-1}
, which proves the claim.

We define two natural bijections between the sets
\mathcal{D}_{\mathbb{P}_{{\rm asym}}^{-1}}(\lambda,2k)\cup\mathcal{D}_{\mathbb{P}_{{\rm asym}}^{-1}}(\lambda,2k-2)
and
\mathcal{U}_{\mathbb{P}_{{\rm asym}}^{-1}}(\lambda,2k)
denoted by
\F_{\mathbb{P}_{{\rm asym}}^{-1},\lambda,2k}^{{\rm row}*}
and
\F_{\mathbb{P}_{{\rm asym}}^{-1},\lambda,2k}^{{\rm col}*}
. For
\mathbf{R}^{-1}_{\lambda}=\{r_{1},\ldots,r_{n}\}
and
\mathbf{S}^{-1}_{\lambda}=\{s_{0},\ldots,s_{n}\}
these are defined as

\F_{\mathbb{P}_{{\rm asym}}^{-1},\lambda,2k}^{{\rm row}*}:\{r_{i_{1}},\ldots,r_{i_{j}}\}\mapsto\begin{cases}\{s_{i_{1}},\ldots,s_{i_{j}}\}\quad&\text{if }j=k,\\
\{s_{0},s_{i_{1}},\ldots,s_{i_{j}}\}&\text{if }j=k-1,\end{cases}

and

\F_{\mathbb{P}_{{\rm asym}}^{-1},\lambda,2k}^{{\rm col}*}:\{r_{i_{1}},\ldots,r_{i_{j}}\}\mapsto\begin{cases}\{s_{i_{1}-1},\ldots,s_{i_{j}-1}\}\quad&\text{if }j=k,\\
\{s_{i_{1}-1},\ldots,s_{i_{j}-1},s_{n}\}&\text{if }j=k-1,\end{cases}

where we identified the partitions
\mu,\nu
with the corresponding sets
\mathbf{R}_{\lambda}^{-1}(\mu)
and
\mathbf{S}_{\lambda}^{-1}(\nu)
.

3.2.
Triangular growths

In this Section we adapt Fomin’s growth diagrams to our framework. Instead of symmetric matrices we use “upper triangular” arrays.

Definition 3.2
.

Let
C=(c_{i,j})_{1\leq i\leq j\leq n}
be a triangular array with non-negative integer entries. A
triangular growth associated with
C
is an assignment
\Lambda=(\Lambda_{i,j})
of partitions to the vertices
(i,j)\in[0,n]\times[0,m]
with
i\leq j
such that

•

for
i<n
holds
\Lambda_{i,j}\prec\Lambda_{i+1,j}
, i.e.,
\Lambda_{i+1,j}/\Lambda_{i,j}
is a horizontal strip,

•

for
j<m
holds
\Lambda_{i,j}\prec\Lambda_{i,j+1}
, i.e.,
\Lambda_{i,j+1}/\Lambda_{i,j}
is a horizontal strip,

•

the size
|\Lambda_{i,j}|
is equal to

(44)

|\Lambda_{i,j}|=\sum_{1\leq k<l\leq i}c_{k,l}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq k\leq i\\
k\leq l\leq j\end{subarray}}c_{k,l}.

We say that
\Lambda
is a
triangular dual growth
, if
\Lambda_{i+1,j}/\Lambda_{i,j}
is a vertical strip instead of a horizontal strip. Since
C
is determined by
\Lambda
we further omit referring to it.

.

The triangular array
C=\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&0&1\\

&&0\end{array}

By extending the local (dual) growth rules as shown next, we can always choose a unique triangular growth from
C
to
P
.

Definition 3.4
.

Let
X
be a set of partitions and
I_{X}(\lambda,k)\subseteq[0,k]
for all
\lambda\in\mathbb{P}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
. A set of
local triangular growth rules

\F_{\bullet}
is a set of local growth rules
\F_{\bullet}
together with a family of bijections

\F_{X,\lambda,k}:\bigcup_{i\in I_{X}(\lambda,k)}\mathcal{D}_{X}(\lambda,i)\rightarrow\mathcal{U}_{X}(\lambda,k),

for each
\lambda\in\mathbb{P}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Analogously a set of
local triangular dual growth rules

\F_{\bullet}^{*}
is a set of local dual growth rules
\F_{\bullet}^{*}
together with a family of bijections

\F_{X,\lambda,k}^{*}:\bigcup_{i\in I_{X}(\lambda,k)}\mathcal{D}_{X}^{*}(\lambda,i)\rightarrow\mathcal{U}_{X}(\lambda,k),

for each
\lambda\in\mathbb{P}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

We have already seen local triangular growth rules implicitly in the previous section. For example Section
3.1.1
considers the case
X=\mathbb{P}
and
I_{\mathbb{P}}(\lambda,k)=[0,k]
and Section
3.1.2
the case
X=\mathbb{P}_{e}
and
I_{\mathbb{P}_{e}}(\lambda,2k+{\rm odd}(\lambda))=\{{\rm odd}(\lambda),{\rm odd}(\lambda)+2,\ldots,{\rm odd}(\lambda)+2k\}
and
I_{\mathbb{P}_{e}}(\lambda,2k+1+{\rm odd}(\lambda))=\emptyset
.

Given a set of local triangular (dual) growth rules
\F_{\bullet}
, we call a triangular (dual) growth
\Lambda
a
triangular (dual)
\F_{\bullet}
-growth diagram
if each full square satisfies
\nu=\F_{\lambda,\rho,|(\lambda\cap\rho)/\mu|+a}(\mu)
(resp.,
\nu=\F^{*}_{\lambda,\rho,|(\lambda\cap\rho)/\mu|+a}(\mu)
), where
\mu,\lambda,\rho,\nu,a
are defined as in (
24
) (resp., (
25
)) and each partial square satisfies
\nu=\F_{X,\lambda,|\lambda/\mu|+c}
(resp.,
\nu=\F^{*}_{X,\lambda,|\lambda/\mu|+c}
) where
\lambda,\mu,\nu,c
are defined as on the left (resp., right) of (
45
). As in Section
2.3
the important observation is that each set of local triangular (dual) growth rules
\F_{\bullet}
yields a bijection between the set of arrays
C=(c_{i,j})_{1\leq i\leq j\leq n}
where the non-diagonal entries are either in
\mathbb{N}
or
\{0,1\}
and the diagonal entries are either
0
, in
2\mathbb{N}
or in
\mathbb{N}
respectively
5
5

5

The allowed values for the entries of
C=(c_{i,j})
depend on the explicit situation, i.e., which of the Littlewood identities we want to prove.
, and the set of SSYTs with shapes in
X
and entries at most
n
. Given an array
C
we construct a triangular (dual)
\F_{\bullet}
-growth diagram as before by using the local triangular (dual) growth rules.

Similarly to Section
2.3
we can also translate the local triangular (dual) growth rules
\F_{\bullet}
into an
\F_{\bullet}
-insertion algorithm. Let
C
be a triangular array as before, denote by
T
the tableau obtained after inserting the first
i-1
columns and
\widehat{T}
the tableau obtained by inserting the
i
-th column of
C
into
T
. Then
\widehat{T}
is obtained by the following two steps.

(1)

We
\F_{\bullet}
-insert the first
i-1
entries of the
i
-th column as defined in Lemma
2.10
or
[
8
, Lemma 2.10]
to obtain
\widehat{T}
restricted to entries at most
i-1
.

(2)

We insert an entry
i
in all cells of
\F_{X,\widehat{T}^{(i-1)},k}(T^{(i-1)})/T^{(i-1)}
, where

k=\left|T^{(i-1)}/\widehat{T}^{(i-1)}\right|+A_{i,i}=\sum_{j=1}^{i}A_{j,i}.

Remark 3.5
.

We can use the above translation to insertion algorithms to relate our growth diagram proofs to well known bijections.

(1)

The Littlewood identity (
4
) is proved by Burges “INSERT1” algorithm
[
4
, Section 2]
. Let
(u_{k},v_{k}),(u_{k+1},v_{k+1}),\ldots,(u_{l},v_{l})
be all pairs of the two-line array with
i=u_{k}=\cdots=u_{l}
. It follows by the proof of the theorem of Section 2 of
[
4
, p. 20]
that none of the
u_{j}
will be bumped when inserting these pairs by “INSERT1”. This implies that we can first row insert the
v_{j}
and then add all
u_{j}
, i.e. the entry
i
, at once. Hence this algorithm is equivalent to using triangular growths where we use row insertion for all local growth rules.

(2)

The Littlewood identity (
5
) is usually obtained from the symmetry of RSK, see (
26
).
Since the bijection
\F_{\mathbb{P},\lambda,k}
is defined by
\F_{\mathbb{P},\lambda,k}=\F_{\lambda,\lambda,k}
, it follows that the triangular
\F_{\bullet}
-growth diagrams are equal to the upper half of a regular growth diagram of a symmetric matrix. In particular this covers the classical proof via row or column insertion.

(3)

Burge argues in
[
4
, Section 3]
that the Littlewood identity (
6
) can be obtained by applying column insertion, which he calls “INSERT*”, to a symmetric matrix with even diagonal entries, or equivalently by a simplified insertion algorithm called “INSERT2”. Since the definition of
\F_{\mathbb{P}_{e},\lambda,k}
is based on the local growth rules
\F_{\bullet}
we obtain the same correspondence by the above argument by using triangular growths where we choose column insertion
\F_{\bullet}^{\rm col}
.

(4)

The Littlewood identity (
7
) is proved by Burges “INSERT3” algorithm. Let
(u_{k},v_{k})
,
(u_{k+1},v_{k+1})
,
\ldots,

(u_{l},v_{l})
be all pairs of the two-line array with
i=u_{k}=\cdots=u_{l}
. Since
i>v_{j}
for all
k\leq j\leq l
no
i
entry is bumped while inserting these pairs via “INSERT3”. Hence we can equivalently first insert
v_{k},v_{k+1},\ldots,v_{l}
as defined by “INSERT3” and then insert all
i
entries. By definition, inserting
v_{k},v_{k+1},\ldots,v_{l}
via “INSERT3” corresponds to the dual row insertion
\F_{\bullet}^{{\rm row}*}
since dual row insertion is reverse traceable. It is immediate by the definition of “INSERT3” that inserting all
i
entries is equivalent to the definition of
\F_{\mathbb{P}_{\rm asym}^{1},\lambda,k}^{{\rm row}*}
. Hence “INSERT3” is equivalent to using triangular dual growths with dual row insertion
\F_{\bullet}^{{\rm row}*}
and
\F_{\mathbb{P}_{\rm asym}^{1},\lambda,k}^{{\rm row}*}
respectively.

(5)

Burge proves the Littlewood identity (
8
) by the “INSERT4” algorithm. As above, we can for each column first insert the
v_{j}
entries and then the
u_{j}
. By definition of “INSERT4” the
v_{j}
are inserted by dual column insertion. Furthermore it is immediate that inserting the
u_{j}
entries is equal to applying
\F_{\mathbb{P}_{\rm asym}^{-1},\lambda,k}^{{\rm row}*}
. Hence Burge’s “INSERT4” algorithm is equivalent to using triangular dual growth diagrams together with dual column insertion
\F_{\bullet}^{{\rm col}*}
and
\F_{\mathbb{P}_{\rm asym}^{-1},\lambda,k}^{{\rm row}*}
.

Similar to Section
2.4
we can extend the framework of triangular (dual) growth diagrams to obtain bijective proofs for the following skew Littlewood identities, compare also to
[
12
, Ch. I, Section 5, Ex. 27]
.

Theorem 3.6
.

Let
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
be a sequence of variables and let
\lambda
be a partition. Then,

(46)

\displaystyle\sum_{\nu\in\mathbb{P}_{e}^{\prime}}s_{\nu/\lambda}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\frac{1}{1-x_{i}x_{j}}\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{P}_{e}^{\prime}}s_{\lambda/\mu}(\mathbf{x}),

(47)

\displaystyle\sum_{\nu\in\mathbb{P}}s_{\nu/\lambda}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{1-x_{i}}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\frac{1}{1-x_{i}x_{j}}\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{P}}s_{\lambda/\mu}(\mathbf{x}),

(48)

\displaystyle\sum_{\nu\in\mathbb{P}_{e}}s_{\nu/\lambda}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{1-x_{i}^{2}}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\frac{1}{1-x_{i}x_{j}}\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{P}_{e}}s_{\lambda/\mu}(\mathbf{x}),

(49)

\displaystyle\sum_{\nu\in\mathbb{P}_{\rm asym}^{1}}s_{\nu/\lambda}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}(1+x_{i}x_{j})\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{P}_{\rm asym}^{-1}}s_{\lambda^{\prime}/\mu}(\mathbf{x}),

(50)

\displaystyle\sum_{\nu\in\mathbb{P}_{\rm asym}^{-1}}s_{\nu/\lambda}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{n}(1+x_{i}^{2})\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}(1+x_{i}x_{j})\sum_{\mu\in\mathbb{P}_{\rm asym}^{1}}s_{\lambda^{\prime}/\mu}(\mathbf{x}).

More explicitely, for a triangular array
C=(c_{i,j})_{1\leq i\leq j\leq n}
as before and a tableau
S\in{\rm SSYT}_{\lambda/\mu}(n)
(resp.
S\in{\rm SSYT}_{\lambda/\mu^{\prime}}^{*}(n)
) we construct a triangular (dual) growth diagram as before with the only difference that we set
\Lambda_{0,i}=S^{(i)}
for
0\leq i\leq n
instead of
\Lambda_{0,i}=\emptyset
. The following example illustrates this for the case of (
50
).

4.
A general framework

In the final section we describe the general set up for which our new approach yields an algebraic proof of Littlewood type identities.

Let
P
be a ranked poset together with two functions
\psi,\psi^{\perp}:P^{2}\rightarrow K
, where
K
is a field and
\psi(\lambda,\mu)\neq 0
or
\psi^{\perp}(\lambda,\mu)\neq 0
respectively implies
\mu\subseteq\lambda
. Typically we write
\psi_{\lambda/\mu}
and
\psi^{\perp}_{\lambda/\mu}
instead of
\psi(\lambda,\mu)
or
\psi^{\perp}(\lambda,\mu)
.
We define the operators
U_{x}
and
D_{y}
called
up operator
and
down operator
respectively on the
K\llbracket x,y\rrbracket
-module
P\llbracket x,y\rrbracket
of formal power series in
x,y
whose coefficients are finite formal sums of
P
over
K
as

U_{x}\lambda=\sum_{\nu}\psi_{\nu/\lambda}x^{{\rm rk}(\nu)-{\rm rk}(\lambda)}\nu,\qquad\qquad D_{y}\lambda=\sum_{\mu}\psi^{\perp}_{\lambda/\mu}y^{{\rm rk}(\lambda)-{\rm rk}(\mu)}\mu,

for partitions
\lambda
and linearly extend it to
P\llbracket x,y\rrbracket
.
For each pair
\lambda,\mu\in P
and
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
and
\mathbf{y}=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{m})
we define the two polynomials

s_{\lambda/\mu}(\mathbf{x}):=\left\langle U_{x_{n}}\cdots U_{x_{1}}\mu,\lambda\right\rangle,\qquad\qquad s_{\lambda/\mu}^{\perp}(\mathbf{y}):=\left\langle D_{y_{1}}\cdots D_{y_{m}}\lambda,\nu\right\rangle,

where
\left\langle\lambda,\mu\right\rangle:=\delta_{\lambda,\mu}
for any elements
\lambda,\mu\in P
and
\left\langle\cdot,\cdot\right\rangle
is linearly extended in the first component to a
K\llbracket x,y\rrbracket
-linear functional on
P\llbracket x,y\rrbracket
.
Note that the polynomials
s_{\lambda/\mu}(\mathbf{x})
and
s_{\lambda/\mu}^{\perp}(\mathbf{y})
are symmetric polynomials in
\mathbf{x}
or
\mathbf{y}
respectively if the up and down operators satisfy the commutation relation
U_{x_{i}}U_{x_{j}}=U_{x_{j}}U_{x_{i}}
and
D_{y_{i}}D_{y_{j}}=D_{y_{j}}D_{y_{i}}
respectively.

Proposition 4.1
.

(1)

Let
f(x,y)\in K(x,y)
be a rational function. The up and down operator satisfy the commutation relation

(51)

D_{y}U_{x}=f(x,y)U_{x}D_{y},

if and only if the polynomials
s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x})
and
s_{\lambda}^{\perp}(\mathbf{y})
satisfy the Cauchy type identities

(52)

\sum_{\nu}s_{\nu/\rho}(\mathbf{x})s_{\nu/\lambda}^{\perp}(\mathbf{y})=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq n\\
1\leq j\leq m\end{subarray}}f(x_{i},y_{j})\sum_{\mu}s_{\lambda/\mu}(\mathbf{x})s_{\rho/\mu}^{\perp}(\mathbf{y}),

for all
\lambda,\rho\in P
.

(2)

Let
g(x)\in K\llbracket x\rrbracket
be a formal power series and
\varphi:P\llbracket x\rrbracket\rightarrow K\llbracket x\rrbracket
a
K\llbracket x\rrbracket
-linear functional. If the up and down operator satisfy additionally to (
51
) the projection identity

(53)

\varphi\circ U_{x}=g(x)\cdot\varphi\circ D_{x}

then the polynomials
s_{\lambda}(\mathbf{x})
satisfy the identity

(54)

\sum_{\nu}\varphi(\nu)s_{\nu/\lambda}(\mathbf{x})=\prod_{i=1}^{n}g(x_{i})\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}f(x_{i},x_{j})\sum_{\mu}\varphi(\mu)s_{\lambda/\mu}^{\perp}(\mathbf{x}),

for all
\lambda\in P
.

Proof.

The first part is classical and an immediate generalisation of the paragraph after Theorem
2.13
.
For the second part, we have by definition

\sum_{\nu}\varphi(\nu)s_{\nu/\lambda}(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{\nu}\varphi(\nu)\left\langle U_{x_{n}}\cdots U_{x_{1}}\lambda,\nu\right\rangle=\varphi\circ U_{x_{n}}\cdots U_{x_{1}}\lambda\\
=g(x_{n})\cdot\varphi\circ D_{x_{n}}U_{x_{n-1}}\cdots U_{x_{1}}\lambda\\
=\cdots=g(x_{n})\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}f(x_{i},x_{n})\cdot\varphi\circ U_{x_{n-1}}\cdots U_{x_{1}}D_{x_{n}}\lambda\\
=\cdots=\prod_{i=1}^{n}g(x_{i})\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}f(x_{i},y_{j})\cdot\varphi\circ D_{x_{1}}\cdots D_{x_{n}}\lambda\\
=\prod_{i=1}^{n}g(x_{i})\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}f(x_{i},y_{j})\cdot\sum_{\mu}\varphi(\mu)\left\langle D_{x_{1}}\cdots D_{x_{n}}\lambda,\mu\right\rangle\\
=\prod_{i=1}^{n}g(x_{i})\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}f(x_{i},y_{j})\sum_{\nu}\varphi(\nu)s_{\lambda/\nu}^{\perp}(\mathbf{x}).\qed
